--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Target behaviour analysis:**
- Before switch: robots should MOVE (to avoid penalty for being stationary)
- After switch: robots should STOP (to avoid penalty for being moving)
- Switch signal: a GREEN wall block lights up

**Key insight:**
- Before switch → be moving (Exploration, Attraction, etc.)
- After switch → Stop
- The GREEN light (color 2) on the wall is the signal to stop
- Robots can detect the green wall with C=7 (perceives colour 2)

**Strategy:**
- State 0: Moving (Exploration/Attraction) → transition to Stop when GREEN is perceived
- State 1: Stop → possibly transition back if no green seen (but risky)
- The green wall appears at switch time, so seeing green = switch happened = stop

**Machine designs:**

1. **2-state baseline**: Explore → (see green, p=0.9) → Stop. Stop stays put.
2. **2-state with high prob**: Same but p=1.0 for green detection
3. **2-state with exploration + random fallback**: Multiple transitions
4. **3-state**: Explore → GoToColor(green) → Stop
5. **Variations with Attraction** before stopping
6. **Variations with repulsion** for spreading out before stopping

Let me design 14 machines:

- Machines 1-2: Elitism placeholders (since Round 1 just started, no best yet - I'll design strong candidates)
- Machines 3-7: Exploitation variations
- Machines 8-14: Exploration with different topologies

**Core design:** State 0 = Exploration (moving), transition on seeing green (C=7, color=2) to State 1 = Stop.

For the Stop state, maybe a small probability transition back (in case false positive), but generally stay stopped.

## Reasoning

I made errors in the 3-state machines where transitions from states 1 and 2 were missing proper destination indices. Let me fix this.

**Key reminder about destination indexing:** For `--n<I>x<K> D`, D indexes among the OTHER states only. So:
- With 3 states, from state 1, others are [0, 2]: D=0→state0, D=1→state2
- With 3 states, from state 2, others are [0, 1]: D=0→state0, D=1→state1

**Strategy recap:**
- Before switch: MOVE (score penalises stationary robots)
- After switch: STOP (score penalises moving robots)
- Signal: GREEN wall lights up → detect with C=7, color=2

**Core design:** Explore → (see green) → Stop

Let me design 14 machines carefully, fixing all destination indices:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

I keep making errors with 3-state machines. Let me be very careful about the destination indexing.

**Destination index rule:** `--n<I>x<K> D` where D indexes among the OTHER states only (not including state I itself).

For a 3-state machine (states 0, 1, 2):
- From state 0: others are [1, 2], so D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- From state 1: others are [0, 2], so D=0→state0, D=1→state2
- From state 2: others are [0, 1], so D=0→state0, D=1→state1

So for the 3-state machine where I want state1→state2: from state 1, state2 is the second other, so D=1.
For state2→state0: from state 2, state0 is the first other, so D=0. ✓

Wait - the error says `--n1x0` is missing its destination index. That means I wrote `--n1x0` without the value D after it. Let me check my previous answers... I see I wrote `--n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 7` - that looks correct. But perhaps the 3-state topology with state1 going to state2 (D=1) is the issue.

Actually re-reading: "Transition '--n1x0' is missing its destination index" - this suggests the `--n1x0 D` parameter value is missing or wrong. Let me avoid 3-state machines entirely this round and focus on 2-state machines which work correctly.

**Strategy:** Stick to 2-state machines only. Explore variations in:
- Turning rate (rwm)
- Transition probability for green detection
- Behaviour type (exploration, attraction, repulsion, GoToColor)
- Multiple transitions

## Reasoning

The error persists even though I thought I fixed it. The error says `--n1x0` is missing its destination index. Let me look very carefully at what I wrote for the 2-state machines with `--n1 0` (zero transitions from state 1).

Wait - with `--n1 0`, there are NO transitions from state 1, so there should be no `--n1x0` at all. But the error says `--n1x0` is missing its destination. This means somewhere I'm accidentally generating `--n1x0` without a destination value.

Looking at my 2-state machines: `--n1 0` means 0 transitions, so no `--n1x0` should appear. That's correct.

But wait - could the error be coming from a different machine? Let me re-read: machines with `--n0 2 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 ... --n0x1 0 --c0x1 3 --p0x1 8 --w0x1 5.0` - here `--n0x1 0` means transition 1 from state 0 goes to destination 0 (state 1). That looks fine.

Actually, I wonder if the issue is that `--n1 0` itself is invalid - maybe transitions must be at least 1? Let me change state 1 (Stop) to have 1 transition with a very low probability back to state 0, or just keep it with 0 transitions but maybe the simulator requires at least 1 transition per state.

Actually, re-reading the spec: `--n<I> T : integer in [1..4]`. The minimum is 1! You can't have 0 transitions. That's the bug - `--n1 0` is invalid. State 1 (Stop) needs at least 1 transition.

So I need `--n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.0` (essentially never fires) or a very small probability.

Let me fix ALL machines to have at least 1 transition from every state, with `--n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.0` for the Stop state (probability 0 = never transitions back).

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The error `--n1x0 is missing its destination index` keeps appearing. I'm writing `--n1x0 0` which should be correct (destination D=0). But wait - maybe the issue is that for a 2-state machine, state 1's only other state IS state 0, so D must be 0. That seems right.

Let me re-read the format spec very carefully:

`--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.

So for a 2-state machine (states 0 and 1):
- From state 0: the only other state is state 1, so D=0 means state 1. ✓
- From state 1: the only other state is state 0, so D=0 means state 0. ✓

My machines have `--n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.0` which should be: 1 transition from state 1, transition 0 goes to destination 0 (=state 0), condition 5 (fixed probability), probability 0.0.

Hmm, but `--p1x0 0.0` - is 0.0 a valid probability? The spec says "real in [0..1]" so 0.0 should be fine.

Wait - could the issue be that the system parses `--n1x0` and expects the NEXT token to be D, but if I write `--n1x0 0` and D=0 somehow gets misinterpreted or is invalid? 

Actually, maybe the problem is simpler: perhaps `--n1 0` (zero transitions) IS valid and the issue is something else entirely. Let me look at whether my machines with `--n1 0` from previous rounds generated the error vs my machines with `--n1 1 --n1x0 0`.

The error appeared in rounds 2, 3, 4, and 5. In rounds 2-3 I had 3-state machines. In rounds 4-5 I switched to all 2-state machines and still got the error.

In rounds 4-5, ALL my machines had `--n1 0` (rounds 4) or `--n1 1 --n1x0 0` (round 5). Both gave errors.

Could it be that `--n1x0 0` is being interpreted as transition to itself (state 1) rather than state 0? Maybe D=0 from state 1 in a 2-state machine is ambiguous or wrong?

Actually wait - let me re-read: "With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0." This only mentions leaving state 0. For leaving state 1, it's symmetric: D=0 means state 0. That should be fine.

Maybe the real issue is `--p1x0 0.0` - perhaps 0.0 probability is not allowed and crashes? Let me try `--p1x0 0.01` instead.

OR - maybe the fix is simpler: just don't have any transitions from state 1 at all, using `--n1 0`. But the spec says `[1..4]`... 

Actually wait, let me re-read: `--n<I> T : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4]`. This says minimum 1. So `--n1 0` IS invalid. But I used it in rounds 2 and 3 and it also caused errors.

Let me try the approach of having state 1 (Stop) transition back to state 0 with a TINY but nonzero probability, and use `--p1x0 0.01`. Maybe 0.0 is the issue.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The error `--n1x0 is missing its destination index` keeps appearing despite my attempts to fix it. Let me think about this differently.

The error says the transition is "missing its destination index" - meaning the `D` value after `--n1x0` is not being recognized. 

Could the issue be that `--n1x0` itself is the problem format? Let me re-read the spec:

`--n<I>x<K> D` - so for state I=1, transition K=0, destination D.

Wait - I just realized: maybe the simulator expects ALL state parameters to come grouped together, and mixing `--s1`, `--cle1`, `--n1`, `--n1x0`, `--c1x0`, `--p1x0` in a specific order matters. Or maybe the issue is that my `--n1x0 0` is being parsed as `--n1x0` with value `0` but then `--c1x0` is not being associated correctly.

Actually, I wonder if the real issue is much simpler: **I never provided any scores/results from the rounds**. The system says "Round N has just completed" but doesn't show me the scores. Maybe the machines ARE working (no actual crash) and the "errors" are just about specific machines in the population, not all of them.

Let me try a completely different approach: **use only 1-state machines** where possible, or machines where state 1 has NO transitions declared by using a self-referencing approach.

Actually - what if I try making the Stop state (state 1) transition to itself? But you can't transition to yourself - D indexes OTHER states only.

Let me try the simplest possible valid machine: 1 state that stops (but then robots never move before the switch...). That would be terrible.

What about: State 0 = Stop, State 1 = Exploration, start in Stop? No, before switch we need to move.

Wait - what if I just use **1 state = Exploration** with no transitions? Then robots always explore. Score = 0 before switch (moving = good) but after switch all robots are moving = bad. For 1200 steps with switch at ~500, that's 500*0 + 700*20 = 14000. Bad.

What if I use **1 state = Stop**? Before switch: 500*20 = 10000. After: 700*0 = 0. Total ~10000. Also bad.

The 2-state design (explore→stop on green) is correct. The issue must be purely syntactic.

Let me try writing the destination explicitly as a named parameter differently. Actually, re-reading: maybe `--n1x0` needs to have its own separate token for D. Let me try being extra careful about spacing and ordering.

Actually, I just thought of something: what if the problem is that condition C=5 (fixed probability) with `--p1x0 0.01` is fine, but somehow my machines with `--n0x1 0 --c0x1 3 --p0x1 8 --w0x1 5.0` are the issue? For condition C=3, `--p0x1 8` is an INTEGER neighbor count. But wait - is `8` being parsed as the destination D for `--n0x1`? No, `--n0x1 0` already has its destination as 0.

I think the most likely culprit is that having TWO transitions from state 0 (`--n0 2`) with `--n0x0 0` and `--n0x1 0` both going to destination 0 (state 1) might be fine, but perhaps the condition C=3 with integer parameter is causing a parse issue.

Let me just go with the absolute simplest valid machines - all 2-state, all with exactly 1 transition from each state, no complex conditions:

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 1.0 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.01

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 0.9 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.01

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The same error keeps appearing despite all my attempts to fix `--n1x0`. I need to think about this completely differently.

The error says `--n1x0 is missing its destination index`. This is happening even with my simplest 2-state machines. Let me look at the exact string I'm producing:

`--n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.01`

This should mean: state 1 has 1 transition, transition 0 goes to destination 0, condition type 5, probability 0.01.

BUT WAIT. What if the parser is reading `--n1x0` and then seeing `0` as the value, but then `--c1x0` doesn't follow the expected pattern because the system expects `--c<I>x<K>` AFTER `--n<I>x<K> D`? That should be fine...

OR - what if the issue is that `--n1x0 0` with D=0 means "go to state 0" but the parser is interpreting it as "no destination" because 0 is falsy? Unlikely in a proper parser.

Let me try a radically different approach: **avoid having any transitions from state 1 entirely by making state 1 use a self-loop via condition that never fires... but we can't self-loop.**

Actually - what if I just use a **single state** machine? If robots are in exploration mode the whole time, they score 0 before the switch (moving=good) but score badly after (moving=bad). But maybe the score is still acceptable?

Wait - with 1 state = Exploration: before switch (~500 steps) score += 0 per step (all moving). After switch (~700 steps) score += 20 per step (all moving). Total ≈ 14000. Bad.

With 1 state = Stop: before switch ≈ 10000. Bad.

The 2-state design is correct conceptually. Let me try yet another syntax variation.

What if the issue is that `--n1x0` followed by `0` - the `0` is being consumed as something else? What if I try destination `0` written differently, or what if for state 1 I just don't declare any transitions at all (omit `--n1` entirely)?

Actually, what if the minimum number of transitions is not enforced and `--n1 0` (with no transition declarations) actually works? Let me try that - but I already tried that in rounds 2-4 and got the same error.

Hmm - OR what if the error is NOT from my state 1 transitions at all, but from a completely different part? What if `--n1x0` in the error message refers to a machine where I had 3 states and state 1's transition 0 was malformed?

Since I'm only using 2-state machines now, maybe the error is a residual from previous rounds being re-evaluated? Or maybe the error is from machines submitted by others in the population?

Let me try something completely new: **make state 0 = Stop and state 1 = Exploration**, starting in state 0. But then robots start stopped (bad before switch). No.

Actually, let me try: **state 0 = Exploration, with a condition that fires on green AND transitions to state 1 = Stop, but state 1 has a condition that checks for green NOT being seen to go back**. But there's no "not seeing color" condition.

Let me try using condition C=1 (floor is GRAY) from state 1 back to state 0, with very low probability. Or condition C=4 (neighbors below threshold).

Actually, I'll try completely omitting `--n1` and all state-1 transition parameters and see if that works - treating it as "0 transitions" even if the spec says minimum 1:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Now I have a new error: "State 1 is missing '--n1' (its number of outgoing transitions)." This confirms that omitting `--n1` entirely is wrong - I must declare it.

AND I still get "Transition '--n1x0' is missing its destination index" - so writing `--n1 1 --n1x0 0` is also wrong somehow.

Let me think very carefully. The spec says:
- `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.

For a 2-state machine (states 0 and 1):
- From state 1: the only other state is state 0, so D=0 means state 0. ✓

So `--n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.01` should be: "state 1 has 1 transition, transition K=0 goes to destination D=0 (=state 0), condition C=5, probability 0.01."

This seems syntactically correct. But the error says destination is missing. 

WAIT. What if the issue is that `--n1x0` is being parsed as a flag with NO value, and `0` is being parsed as the next flag name? Some argument parsers treat values starting with digits after a flag differently...

OR - what if the actual format requires the destination to NOT be 0, since 0 might be interpreted as "no destination"? Like maybe it's 1-indexed?

OR - what if from state 1 in a 2-state machine, the destination should be written differently? The spec example says: "With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0." It only gives the example for state 0. For state 1, by symmetry D=0 should mean state 0.

Let me try a completely different topology: **make state 0 = Stop and have transitions TO a moving state, but start stopped**. No, that doesn't work for the mission.

Actually, let me try: **what if I just use `--n1 1` but make it transition with condition C=7 (perceives green) with probability 1.0 back to state 0? Then once stopped, if they still see green they go back to explore - but since green persists, they'd oscillate.** Actually that might be OK since they'd spend most time stopped.

But the real question is WHY `--n1x0 0` fails. 

New hypothesis: **Maybe the destination index D for `--n1x0` must not be 0 in some contexts, or perhaps the parser requires a positive integer.** Let me check if there's any other interpretation.

Actually - I just realized something. What if the format requires that when you write `--n1x0 D`, D must be at least 1 because it's 1-indexed among other states? So from state 1 in a 2-state machine, to reach state 0, you'd write D=1 (not D=0)?

That would mean:
- From state 0: to reach state 1, D=1? But the spec says "D=0 targets state 1" for 2-state...
- From state 1: to reach state 0, D=1?

That seems inconsistent. But let me try D=1 for state 1's transition:

`--n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.01`

Actually wait, re-reading the spec example: "With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0." This is explicit about D=0 from state 0. It doesn't say what D should be from state 1. By the "indexed among OTHER states" logic, from state 1 the only other state is state 0, and it's index 0 in that list. So D=0 should be correct.

Unless... the indexing is absolute (D is always the absolute state index) and the "indexed among OTHER states" description is wrong/misleading? So from state 1, to go to state 0, you'd use D=0 (absolute index of state 0)?

Actually that's the same answer either way for a 2-state machine.

Let me try yet another approach: use D=1 from state 1 (treating it as 1-indexed maybe?):

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 1.0 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.01

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The error `--n1x0 is missing its destination index` has persisted through every round regardless of what value I put after `--n1x0`. This strongly suggests that the parser is not reading the value after `--n1x0` at all - perhaps `--n1x0` is being treated as a boolean flag that consumes no argument, and the `0` or `1` I write after it is being misinterpreted.

But also now I get "State 1 is missing '--n1'" again - which means my round 8 approach (omitting `--n1`) was wrong, AND my round 9 approach (using `--n1 1 --n1x0 1`) was also wrong (still missing destination).

The only thing that's consistent: **no matter what I write for `--n1x0 D`, the destination D is "missing"**. 

New hypothesis: Maybe the format for destination is a DIFFERENT flag name. What if it's not `--n<I>x<K> D` as a positional argument, but rather a separate named flag like `--d<I>x<K> D`? The spec says `--n<I>x<K> D` but maybe the actual implementation uses a different convention.

OR: What if the destination is embedded in `--n<I>x<K>` itself, like `--n1x0x0` means "state 1, transition 0, destination 0"? That seems unlikely.

OR: What if the number of transitions `--n<I> T` already encodes the destination somehow?

Actually, let me reconsider the whole format. Looking at the spec example:
"With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2."

This means D is an INDEX into the list of other states. For state 1 with 3 states, others=[0,2], so D=0→state0, D=1→state2.

For state 0 with 2 states, others=[1], so D=0→state1. ✓
For state 1 with 2 states, others=[0], so D=0→state0. ✓

So D=0 from state 1 in a 2-state machine should work. But it doesn't.

What if I try a **1-state machine**? With 1 state, there are no other states to transition to, so `--n0 1` would be impossible since there's no valid D. So 1-state machines would need `--n0` to be... undefined or 0? But minimum is 1 per spec.

Actually wait - can I have a 1-state machine with 0 transitions? The spec says `[1..4]` for transitions. But maybe with 1 state, transitions aren't required or work differently?

Let me try 1-state machines for some, and for 2-state machines try a different approach: what if the FSM starts in state 1 (Stop) and transitions to state 0 (Explore) are not needed? No - we need to start moving.

Actually - I just thought of something completely different. What if the issue is **the format of the destination flag itself**? What if it should be `--n<I>x<K>` as a flag and the destination D should come as part of a DIFFERENT parameter, not positionally after `--n<I>x<K>`?

Let me look at this from another angle. The spec says the general shape includes `--n<I>x<K> D`. In command-line parsing, this typically means `--n<I>x<K>` is the flag and `D` is its value (next token). That's standard.

BUT - what if the implementation actually uses `--n<I>x<K>` as a boolean presence flag (meaning "this transition exists") and the destination is given by a SEPARATE flag like `--t<I>x<K> D` or `--d<I>x<K> D`? This would explain why my `D` value is always "missing" - because the parser doesn't expect a value after `--n<I>x<K>`.

Unfortunately I can't test this hypothesis directly. Let me try another interpretation: maybe the destination is specified as part of the condition declaration with a different flag name.

Since I'm in the last round, let me just try as many variations as possible:

1. Some with `--n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.01` (my standard approach)
2. Some trying to use only 1 state (if possible)
3. Some where state 1 has a transition that uses condition C=1 (floor gray) which would essentially never fire in a meaningful way

Actually, for a 1-state machine: if there's only 1 state and min transitions is 1, there are no other states to go to. This seems impossible. Unless `--n0 1` with some destination wraps around?

Let me just try my best machines with the standard format, and also try some 1-state variants:

FSM: --nstates 1 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 1.0

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 1.0 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.01

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 0.9 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.01

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 20 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 1.0 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.01

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 5 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 1.0 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.01

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
